Title: Concave Roofs and Downward Recovery for DR-Submodular Polynomial Maximization

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.04027v1

Published Time: Tue, 06 Oct 2026 00:18:14 GMT

Number of Pages: 83

Markdown Content:
# Concave Roofs and Downward Recovery for DR-Submodular Polynomial Maximization 

Donglei Du 

Faculty of Management, University of New Brunswick Fredericton, NB E3B 5A3, Canada ddu@unb.ca ORCID: 0000-0003-0111-8572 

Abstract 

We give approximation algorithms for max {𝐹 (𝑥 ) : 𝑥 ∈ K} , where K is a nonempty compact down-closed convex subset of the unit cube and 𝐹 is a nonnegative diminishing-returns (DR) submodular polynomial: every entry of its Hessian is nonpositive on the cube. Starting from quadratic or cubic coefficients, we decompose the objective exactly into nonnegative rooted atoms and affine terms. Concave upper bounds for these components give a tractable roof. We then decrease the coordinates of a roof optimizer using maps that work for all components at once, recovering a fraction of the roof value while preserving feasibility. For quadratics, we also retain the concave diagonal residual exactly. Under the stated assumptions on optimization access, these constructions give deterministic polynomial-time factors of 1 /2 for quadratics and 8 /17 for cubics, up to any prescribed additive error. The cubic result includes positive cubic coefficients and repeated variables. The factor 8 /17 also holds for two larger classes: supplied compatible rooted certificates of literal degree at most twelve, and sums of nonnegative submodular local functions of arity at most four. At arbitrary finite literal degree, a common scalar map attains the optimal single-map factor 2 √3 − 3. Global monotonicity and concave-cardinality structure give stronger guarantees for multilinear local-function sums, with no recovery beyond the identity map. Standard matroid rounding transfers the multilinear guarantees without loss. We also prove online preservation for directed-hypergraph cuts and, separately, a Unique-Games inapproximability bound of approximately 0 .82843 for explicit quadratic input under a cardinality upper bound. 

Keywords: DR-submodularity; submodular maximization; concave roof; concave closure; nonnegative decomposition; approximation; online contention resolution; explicit-input hardness. 

Mathematics Subject Classification (2020): 90C26, 90C27, 90C30, 68Q17, 68W25.

# 1 Introduction 

We consider OPT (𝐹, K) = max {𝐹 (𝑥 ) : 𝑥 ∈ K} , (1.1) where K ⊆ [ 0, 1]𝑛 is nonempty, compact, convex, and down-closed : 𝑥 ∈ K and 0 ≤ 𝑦 ≤ 𝑥 imply 𝑦 ∈ K . A polynomial is Boolean-nonnegative when it is nonnegative at every Boolean vertex. It is diminishing-returns (DR) submodular when all diagonal and mixed second partial derivatives are nonpositive on the cube. In particular, it is concave in each coordinate separately, so Boolean nonnegativity implies nonnegativity throughout the cube. We maximize a tractable concave upper bound and recover a feasible point whose objective value is a fixed fraction of that bound. When components share variables, recovering them separately may require conflicting choices. We resolve this conflict with maps that decrease coordinates and work for all components at once. 

1.1 Two coefficient-input algorithms 

For Boolean-nonnegative quadratic DR polynomials, we decompose the multilinear part into nonnegative components and keep the concave diagonal residual intact. Clipping then recovers half the roof value while preserving feasibility, giving a deterministic 1 /2 approximation. Our second result is a deterministic 8 /17 approximation for every Boolean-nonnegative cubic DR polynomial. Two difficulties arise beyond the quadratic case. DR-submodularity permits positive cubic coefficients, and replacing every positive coordinate exponent by one (Boolean multilinearization) can leave a residual that is not concave. We address both with an exact complement identity and a linear program for the decomposition. Two common maps then balance the competing recovery requirements of the components. Both algorithms start from rational coefficients and incur only a prescribed additive error under the access assumptions in Section 2.5. To handle repetitions, Section 4.6 proves the scalar inequalities before identifying coordinates. Table 1: The two coefficient-input guarantees. Each algorithm constructs its representation and returns 𝑦 ∈ K 

with 𝐹 (𝑦 ) ≥ 𝜌 OPT (𝐹, K) − 𝜀 . Powers and repeated coordinates are retained.        

> Objective Factor 𝜌 Construction Recovery Quadratic DR polyno-mial 1/2Pair orientation and exact concave diagonal residual One clipping map; Theorem 3.1 Cubic DR polynomial 8/17 Complement identity and uniform-polarity orientation Two scalar maps; Theorem 4.5

Boolean values alone do not determine the continuous objective. For example, 𝑡 (1 − 𝑡 ) vanishes at both Boolean points but has a positive interior optimum. A multilinear extension therefore cannot replace an arbitrary continuous polynomial. Section 2.2 makes this distinction precise. Section 4.8 follows one constrained continuous cubic from its coefficients to a recovered point and compares the continuous optimum, the Boolean optimum, and the roof bound. 

1.2 The mechanism and its extensions 

A concave roof is a concave majorant of the objective. A literal is a coordinate or its complement. A rooted atom subtracts a literal product from one distinguished factor, its root; the other occurrences are its heads. A 

rooted certificate is an exact nonnegative sum of atoms, literals, and a constant. Orientation allocates each 5Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du 

subtracted product’s coefficient among its permitted root choices. Literal degree counts occurrences, including repetitions. The low-degree construction uses uniform-polarity products, consisting only of coordinates or only of complements. The more general compatibility rule requires a complemented root whenever the product contains a complemented literal. A downward map satisfies 0 ≤ 𝑅 (𝑥 ) ≤ 𝑥 . A portfolio is a finite collection of downward maps with nonnegative weights summing to one. A common-coordinatewise map uses the same scalar function on every coordinate. The recovery inequalities add because every component uses the same maps and weights, including components with overlapping supports. Termwise recovery requires a separate inequality for each generator. The algorithm evaluates all branches and keeps the one with the largest original objective value. Theorem 2.3 gives the guarantee; Algorithm 1 specifies how to allocate numerical error. The extensions in Table 2 go in two directions. Larger rooted certificates allow more literal occurrences, while richer local functions allow any nonnegative submodular function on a small support. A local function’s 

arity counts distinct coordinates. Its concave closure maximizes expected value over joint distributions with prescribed coordinate marginals. Recentering subtracts an affine function from each local component to make its remaining marginals nonnegative. Table 2: Extensions and structural refinements. Weights are nonnegative and representations are supplied unless a construction is stated. The local-function rows concern multilinear extensions.                       

> Additional input structure Factor Additional argument Compatible rooted certificate of literal degree at most twelve 8/17 Higher-rank atom inequalities; Section 5 Compatible rooted certificate of arbitrary finite literal degree 2√3−3An optimal common scalar map; Theorem 5.4 Nonnegative submodular local functions of arity at most four 8/17 A different pair on the full local cone; Section 6 Globally monotone sum of nonnegative submodular local functions of arity at most
> 𝑑 ≥11− ( 1−1/𝑑 )𝑑 Recentering; local monotonicity is unnecessary Nonnegative concave-cardinality local functions of arities 𝑑 𝑏 ≥2min 𝑏 [1− ( 1−1/𝑑 𝑏 )𝑑 𝑏 −1]Exact cardinality closure and binomial comparison

The degree-twelve atom inequalities are formalized in Lean. The arity-four inequalities are certified by exact arithmetic on a finite generating family. The exhibited map pairs differ, but the scalar map with factor 2√3 − 3 works for both families and their nonnegative sums. We construct certificates for higher-degree negatively signed polynomials. The remaining higher-degree guarantees assume that a compatible certificate is supplied. 

Boundaries and stronger roofs. The quadratic and cubic examples also reveal a limit of downward recovery: a roof optimizer may set to zero a coordinate needed by a better feasible solution. Section 8 proves sharpness for specified fixed roofs, representation families, and additive local roofs of arity at most four. For multilinear objectives whose representations may be optimized, fractional triple packing captures the remaining freedom in the coefficients; triangle-free pair capacities eliminate that freedom. At literal degree thirteen, a complemented atom conflicts with the two-point equality structure of the cubic witness. This yields a strict supremum gap for finite common-coordinatewise termwise portfolios. Table 3 records the roof, benchmark, and recovery class to which each claim applies. Grouping local functions forces their joint distributions to agree within each group and can tighten the roof. Section 9 develops these formulations, finite games for choosing recovery maps, and recovery adapted to a supplied instance. 6Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du 

Discrete, online, and computational consequences. For a nonnegative submodular set function 𝑓 and a down-closed feasible family I, the discrete problem is max { 𝑓 (𝑆 ) : 𝑆 ∈ I} . (1.2) Its multilinear extension belongs to the continuous model. Standard matroid rounding converts the applicable fractional guarantees without loss, although this conversion can fail for general continuous polynomials. The online theorem uses a more specific representation by directed-hypergraph cuts and positive association. To obtain a prophet guarantee, we also need the outcome-space benchmark in Appendix K. Sections 12–13 address computational hardness for explicit rational quadratic input. Padding transfers the established 12 /13 NP-hardness bound to cardinality density 1 /2. A heterogeneous directed test and an affine Gaussian certificate give a Unique-Games inapproximability bound of 0 .828426578 . . . at asymptotic density 0.0933003645. These bounds apply to deterministic polynomial-time algorithms and leave a gap above our quadratic factor 1 /2. 

1.3 Relation to prior work 

Independently obtained results. The quadratic 1 /2 and cubic 8 /17 guarantees were obtained independently in this work and by Aggarwal [3]. Both approaches combine nonnegative decompositions, concave majorants, and common recovery maps for low-degree objectives. Aggarwal also develops geometry-dependent refinements and adversarial-online guarantees. Here we construct the orientation by linear programming and identify its optimal value through threshold sets in the dual. We retain repeated powers through an exact complement identity on the original cube and obtain the baseline cubic guarantee with two explicit maps. Continuous DR-submodularity combines lattice submodularity with coordinatewise concavity [10, 11, 53, 55]. Multilinear relaxation, continuous greedy, and measured continuous greedy provide general algorithms for submodular maximization [18, 20, 28]. The 0 .401 guarantee of Buchbinder and Feldman [19] applies to nonnegative smooth DR-submodular objectives under their assumptions on smoothness and optimization access. Our larger ratios rely on the additional structure of the objective classes considered here. 

Oracle barriers and the role of structure. Oveis Gharan and Vondrák proved value-oracle barriers of 0.478 under a matroid independence constraint and 0 .491 under a cardinality constraint [46]. Qi strengthened the cardinality barrier to 0 .478 [48, Theorem 4.1]. These are exponential value-query lower bounds for unrestricted nonnegative submodular functions. They do not directly impose the same bounds on explicitly represented fixed-degree polynomials or objectives supplied with the decompositions used here. Under a fixed-degree promise, a multilinear polynomial can be reconstructed with polynomially many value queries; the bit-length qualification and the limitation to multilinear objectives are stated in Section 2.5. In particular, the general 0 .478 barrier does not establish near-optimality of 8 /17 for the intrinsic cubic class. Three kinds of limitation must be kept separate: the information available from a value oracle, the factor recoverable from a given roof under a given rule, and polynomial-time hardness for explicitly encoded instances. Oracle bounds motivate structural assumptions. Recovery bounds describe the limits of a method. Computational hardness constrains algorithms whether or not they use a roof. We also distinguish matroid-base constraints, which are not down-closed, from the matroid-independence constraints used here. Negaforms, roof duality, and concave extensions have a long history in pseudo-Boolean optimization [14, 15, 24, 25, 33–35, 41]. The cubic unary-negaform representation, in which each nonlinear product uses only variables or only complements, is due to Billionnet and Minoux [12]; see also Boros and Gruber [13]. Xu and Liberti [59] study relaxations based on signed certificates. Our construction retains the exact polynomial identity on the continuous cube. An orientation linear program produces nonnegative rooted coefficients, and common downward maps recover value from the resulting roof. 7Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du 

Quadratic optimization. The pairwise linear roof used in the quadratic construction belongs to the classical bilinear-relaxation framework. Section 3.6 compares it with semidefinite relaxations and explains which additional feasibility and objective assumptions those methods use. For box constraints, Niazadeh et al. [45] obtain a randomized 1 /2 approximation, up to additive error, for general nonnegative Lipschitz continuous lattice-submodular objectives. Section 3.4 gives a direct quadratic specialization: replace positive squares by endpoint chords, apply the DR quadratic algorithm, and choose endpoints in those coordinates without further loss. Concave closures and correlation gaps compare correlated and independent distributions [4, 23, 38, 60]. For nonmonotone functions, the max-subset comparison of Rubinstein and Singla [51] permits deletions, and the downward comparison of Chekuri and Livanos [21] permits decreases of the marginals. Local closures introduce a further difficulty: optimal joint distributions for different components may disagree on shared coordinates. Grouping enforces some of the missing consistency; a common map ensures that the recovery inequalities can be added. For monotone objectives, the constants 1 − ( 1 − 1/𝑑 )𝑑 follow from the finite-ground-set correlation inequality of Chekuri et al. [23, Lemma 4.10]. Coverage relaxations and pipage rounding give the coverage specializations [1]. Our recentering argument requires monotonicity only of the sum and preserves the local ground sets. Concave-coverage refinements and curvature bounds use other structural assumptions [6, 54]. Monotonic decompositions of submodular functions are studied by Bérczi et al. [7]. Offline contention resolution [23], greedy online contention resolution [29], and submodular dominance [49] provide different ways to round fractional solutions. Our online preservation proof applies Harris’s inequality to directed-hyperedge cuts [36]. The offline hypergraph application uses the more general splitting functions of Veldt et al. [57]; the online theorem requires the specific cut representation. 

Explicit-input hardness. For unconstrained Max-DiCut, Trevisan, Sorkin, Sudan, and Williamson proved NP-hardness within 12 /13 + 𝜀 [56]. Brakensiek et al. give an unconditional 0 .87448-approximation and, assuming Khot’s Unique Games Conjecture (UGC) [39], 0 .87461-hardness for unconstrained Max-DiCut [16]. Padding transfers either hardness bound to the at-most model at density 1 /2; these unconstrained bounds do not determine the fixed-density approximability studied here. Approximation frameworks for Boolean constraint-satisfaction problems with exact global-cardinality constraints were developed through semidefinite-programming hierarchies and reductions based on coordinate-selection tests by Raghavendra and Tan [50]. Their algorithmic methodology treats exact cardinality, whereas our recovery setting uses down-closed constraints. Austrin and Stanković showed that prescribed cardinality can strengthen UGC-inapproximability, obtaining the approximately 0 .858 boundary for cardinality-constrained Max-Cut [5]. For directed cuts with a prescribed part size, Ageev, Hassin, and Sviridenko established the classical 1 /2-approximation benchmark [2]; enumerating all prescribed sizes 𝑝 ≤ 𝑘 transfers that guarantee to an upper bound |𝑆 | ≤ 𝑘 . Ghuge et al. also give a direct 1 /2-approximation for weighted Max-DiCut with nonnegative arc weights under an at-most-cardinality constraint [31]. The present hardness test directly treats this at-most model and uses heterogeneous biases to preserve directed asymmetry. The analytic soundness step uses Gaussian noise-correlation bounds [43]. The finite certificate uses Plackett’s reduction formula for multivariate normal probabilities [47] and the correctly rounded multiple-precision floating-point library MPFR [30]. We also evaluate the generic UGC-to-NP “losing-half” transfer of Bhangale and Khot [9]; for the certified test, the available soundness certificate does not preserve a gap. 

1.4 Organization and proof routes 

Part I develops the quadratic and cubic algorithms. Section 2 defines the continuous and multilinear models and states the common-recovery guarantee. Section 3 gives the quadratic construction, clipping proof, 8Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du 

applications, and first obstruction. Section 4 handles the additional cubic difficulties, proves the two-map guarantee, and works through a continuous example. Appendix A supplies the remaining scalar analysis. Part II treats larger certificates, richer local functions, and stronger structural assumptions in Sections 5–7. It then establishes recovery limits, develops stronger roofs and recovery rules, and gives the discrete and correlation consequences. Part III proves online preservation; Appendix K gives the outcome-space argument. Part IV presents the separate computational-hardness results and the remaining questions. Appendices C, D, and H document the three proof-software components. 

# Part I 

# Quadratic and cubic coefficient-input algorithms 

# 2 Continuous and multilinear models; common recovery 

We first distinguish a continuous polynomial from the multilinear interpolation of its Boolean values. A two-edge example then shows why overlapping components need a common recovery rule.

2.2 Restriction, interpolation, and relaxation 

Let 𝐹 be a continuous polynomial, let 𝑓 (𝑆 ) = 𝐹 (1𝑆 ), and write 𝑀 for the multilinear extension of 𝑓 . Passing from 𝐹 to 𝑀 is Boolean multilinearization : replace every positive coordinate exponent by one and collect coefficients. Three operations must be distinguished: evaluating 𝐹 at Boolean points, interpolating those values, and relaxing an integrality constraint. An exact polynomial identity holds throughout the cube, before imposing 𝑥 2 

> 𝑖

= 𝑥 𝑖 . Exactness of a representation does not imply that its objective can be optimized exactly. For a DR polynomial, Lemma 2.1 gives 𝑀 ≤ 𝐹 , with equality throughout the cube precisely when 𝐹 is multilinear. We also have 𝑀 ≤ 𝑓 +, since independent sampling is an admissible coupling. The functions 𝐹 

and 𝑓 + have no general ordering: the next example has 𝐹 > 𝑓 + in the interior, whereas the directed edge below has 𝐹 < 𝑓 + at the midpoint. 

Example 2.2 (A continuous roof is not necessarily a Boolean concave closure) . Let 𝐹 (𝑡 ) = 𝑡 (1 − 𝑡 ). Its Boolean restriction and the multilinear extension of that restriction are identically zero. Its standard rooted roof is 𝐿 (𝑡 ) = min {𝑡, 1 − 𝑡 }, while 𝐹 (1/2) = 1/4. The continuous concave envelope , the smallest concave majorant on the interval, is 𝐹 itself because 𝐹 is concave. Thus the Boolean concave closure, the continuous concave envelope, and the standard roof are three different functions. In particular, a Boolean closure need not majorize a continuous objective. 10 Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du

2.3 One edge and a shared-coordinate conflict 

For a directed edge with distinct endpoints, 

ℎ(𝑥 1, 𝑥 2) = 𝑥 1 (1 − 𝑥 2), ℓ(𝑥 1, 𝑥 2) = min {𝑥 1, 1 − 𝑥 2}.

The product is bounded by each factor, so their minimum is a concave majorant. Here ℓ is also the concave closure of the Boolean edge function: a coupling can make the intersection of the two relevant events attain the smaller marginal. At (1/2, 1/2), independent sampling gives 1 /4, while the coupling assigning probability 1/2 to each of (1, 0) and (0, 1) gives closure value 1 /2. Now consider the edges 1 → 2 and 2 → 3. Maximizing their contributions separately below 

(1/2, 1/2, 1/2) requires 𝑦 2 = 0 and 𝑦 2 = 1/2, respectively. No single point meets both requirements. A common recovery rule makes both component inequalities refer to the same vector, allowing us to add them. 

2.4 The upper-bound interface 

A map 𝑅 : [0, 1] 𝑁 → [ 0, 1] 𝑁 is downward if 0 ≤ 𝑅 (𝑥 ) ≤ 𝑥 . A portfolio is a finite family of downward maps with nonnegative weights summing to one; each map is a branch. A common-coordinatewise map applies the same scalar function 𝜙 to every coordinate: 𝑅 (𝑥 )𝑖 = 𝜙 (𝑥 𝑖 ). A common map uses one global output in every component, including components that overlap. Downwardness preserves every down-closed set containing 

𝑥 . It is also necessary for this universal feasibility property, as the set [0, 𝑥 ] shows. A map tailored to one feasible region may satisfy weaker conditions.

Multiply the component inequalities by 𝑤 𝑏 and sum. Nonnegative weights preserve their direction, and the same global map in each summand makes the left sides add to 𝐹 (𝑅 𝑠 (𝑥 )) . □

For a fixed objective, componentwise recovery is sufficient but may be stronger than necessary, since weighted deficits can cancel. 

Certificate terminology used in the low-degree theorems. A literal is 𝑥 𝑖 or 1 − 𝑥 𝑖 ; these are also called unary terms. A rooted atom is a root literal minus a product containing it, with repeated occurrences permitted; the other occurrences are its heads. It is nonnegative on the cube. Its standard atom roof is the minimum of the root literal and the sum of the complements of its head literals. An exact negaform writes the objective as an affine function minus a nonnegative sum of literal products. A nonnegative rooted certificate is a finite exact identity consisting of a nonnegative constant, nonnegative unary terms, and a nonnegative sum of rooted atoms. It is compatible when every product containing a complemented literal has a complemented root, and every positive-only product has a positive root. The literal degree counts occurrences in the product. Write ℭ≤𝑑 for the cone of finite nonnegative combinations of 1, literals, and compatible atoms of literal degree at most 𝑑 . Here a cone generated by a family consists of its finite nonnegative linear combinations. Definition 5.1 gives the occurrence-indexed form used for larger certificates. The intrinsic quadratic and cubic constructions use only uniform-polarity products: all coordinates or all complements. For a rooted certificate, positive unaries require Í𝑠 𝑝 𝑠 [𝑅 𝑠 (𝑥 )] 𝑖 ≥ 𝜌𝑥 𝑖 . Complemented unaries lose no value because 1 − [ 𝑅 𝑠 (𝑥 )] 𝑖 ≥ 1 − 𝑥 𝑖 ; a nonnegative constant also needs no additional condition when 𝜌 ≤ 1. We say that a map or portfolio pays a component by factor 𝜌 when it satisfies (2.6) for that component. Thus atomwise payment and the positive-unary inequality suffice. 

2.5 Input and optimization access 

We supply continuous polynomials as sparse lists of rational coefficients and exponents, and measure complexity in the total binary encoding length. The low-degree algorithms construct their certificates from these coefficients. Later sections specify the encodings needed for supplied certificates, local tables, and cardinality sequences. For fixed multilinear degree 𝑑 , Möbius inversion uses only values on sets of size at most 𝑑 . We can therefore recover the polynomial with 𝑛 𝑂 (𝑑 ) exact value queries and arithmetic operations. A polynomial bound on bit complexity also requires polynomially bounded encoding lengths for the returned rational values. Boolean queries alone cannot recover a general continuous polynomial. 12 Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du 

For 𝛿 ≥ 0, a 𝛿 -accurate maximizer of a function 𝐻 over K is a feasible point ¯ 𝑥 with 𝐻 ( ¯𝑥 ) ≥ max K 𝐻 − 𝛿 .The analytical guarantees apply whenever we can obtain such a roof optimizer. For unconditional polynomial-time implementations, we use a rational polytope K with an explicit polynomial-size inequality system, or a polynomially bounded rational-polytope encoding together with a polynomial-time strong-separation oracle. On a queried rational point, this oracle either confirms membership or returns a rational hyperplane that strictly separates the point from the polytope [52]. The coefficient-input algorithms have polynomial-size lifted roofs. After reversing the sign, the quadratic hybrid roof gives a convex quadratic program. For a general compact convex set, we assume a procedure that returns a feasible roof optimizer with the prescribed additive accuracy. A rooted atom roof uses one auxiliary variable constrained below its two affine bounds. The quadratic hybrid roof retains the concave diagonal residual; the cubic roof is piecewise affine. Section 4.10 gives the formulation sizes. We evaluate powers with binary exponents by repeated squaring and controlled rounding. Approximating fixed algebraic maps coordinatewise downward preserves feasibility. The following continuity bound controls the error in the objective value. A sparse polynomial 𝐹 (𝑥 ) = Í𝛽 𝑐 𝛽 𝑥 𝛽 satisfies 

|𝐹 (𝑥 ) − 𝐹 (𝑦 )| ≤ 𝑀 𝐹 ∥𝑥 − 𝑦 ∥∞, 𝑀 𝐹 =∑︁ 

> 𝛽

|𝑐 𝛽 | | 𝛽 | (𝑥, 𝑦 ∈ [ 0, 1] 𝑁 ).

The bound follows by integrating the first-derivative bounds along the segment from 𝑥 to 𝑦 . For a certificate, an analogous bound is the sum of each atom coefficient times one plus its literal degree, together with the unary contributions. The bound has polynomial logarithmic encoding length even when multiplicities are given in binary. We can therefore round an algebraic recovered point downward to a rational point with any prescribed additive loss. If each branch value is evaluated within 𝜂 , choosing the largest estimate loses at most 2 𝜂 . Thus the implementation does not require exact comparisons of algebraic numbers. 

# 3 Quadratics: a complete roof-and-recovery algorithm 

The quadratic algorithm has three steps: separate the diagonal terms, orient the pair coefficients into nonnegative atoms, and clip a roof optimizer to obtain a feasible point. The oriented coefficients also give a directed-cut representation. An odd cycle will show why downward recovery from this roof cannot guarantee more than one-half.

Add the exact diagonal residual from (3.2) to obtain the algorithm’s concave upper bound. The edge terms admit a linear hypograph formulation; the diagonal terms are concave quadratics. Define 𝜙 2 (𝑡 ) = min {𝑡, 1/2}. For 𝑎, 𝑢 ∈ [ 0, 1],

𝜙 2 (𝑎 ) ≥ 𝑎 /2, 𝜙 2 (𝑎 )( 1 − 𝜙 2 (𝑢 )) ≥ 12 min {𝑎, 1 − 𝑢 }. (3.6) If 𝑎 ≤ 1/2, the product is at least 𝑎 /2; otherwise it is at least (1 − 𝑢 )/ 2. Interchanging root and head gives the complemented one-head inequality, also when both occurrences refer to the same coordinate. 

Theorem 3.1 (Quadratic half approximation) . Every Boolean-nonnegative quadratic DR polynomial (3.1) 

admits a deterministic 1/2 approximation over K, up to any prescribed additive error, under Section 2.5. The same factor applies to every supplied degree-two compatible rooted certificate with its standard roof. Proof. Let 𝐿 𝑀 be the standard roof of the certificate for 𝑀 in (3.2). Optimize the hybrid concave roof

> 𝑖

to additive accuracy 𝛿 , and put 𝑦 𝑖 = 𝜙 2 ( ¯𝑥 𝑖 ). Equation (3.6) pays each rooted quadratic term and positive unary by 1 /2; constants and complemented unaries lose no value. Clipping also preserves the diagonal residual: if ¯𝑥 𝑖 ≤ 1/2, then 𝑦 𝑖 = ¯𝑥 𝑖 ; otherwise

For a supplied degree-two compatible certificate, the positive and complemented uniform-polarity atoms satisfy the same clipping inequality. A mixed atom has the form (1 − 𝑎 )( 1 − 𝑢 ), including identified coordinates, and roof min {1 − 𝑎, 1 − 𝑢 }. Both recovered factors are at least 1 /2 and dominate their original values, so their product is at least half this roof. Constants and complemented unaries lose no value, so aggregation gives the same factor for its standard roof. □

The diagonal-free sharpness examples in Section 8 apply both to the hybrid roof and to the standard atom roof. 

The implemented quadratic algorithm. Construct (3.5), maximize the hybrid roof to the accuracy specified in Section 2.5, and return 𝑦 𝑖 = min { ¯𝑥 𝑖 , 1/2}. Negating the roof gives a convex quadratic program; when the diagonal residual vanishes, the formulation is linear. Algorithm 1 specifies the numerical tolerances.

This positive affine change preserves degree, mixed derivative signs, and nonnegativity. Work with the normalized polynomial and its surrogate (3.8). The algorithm therefore consists of the existing orientation LP, concave quadratic roof optimization, clipping, and one pass of endpoint choices. Form 𝑍 by independently replacing each 𝑦 𝑖 , 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼 +, with a Bernoulli variable of mean 𝑦 𝑖 , leaving the other coordinates fixed. Then E𝐹 (𝑍 ) = 𝐺 (𝑦 ), and the endpoint procedure makes this rounding deterministic. Endpoint choices can increase coordinates; their feasibility uses the product structure of the box. 

Example 3.3 (A convex coordinate and a fractional optimum) . On [0, 1]2, take 

𝐹 (𝑥 1, 𝑥 2) = 𝑥 21 + ( 1 − 𝑥 1)𝑥 2 + 𝑥 2 (1 − 𝑥 2).

This function is nonnegative and lattice submodular, while 𝜕 211 𝐹 = 2 > 0. Replacing 𝑥 21 by 𝑥 1 gives 

𝐺 = 𝑥 1 (1 − 𝑥 2) + 𝑥 2 + 𝑥 2 (1 − 𝑥 2), with hybrid roof 

𝐻 𝐺 (𝑥 ) = min {𝑥 1, 1 − 𝑥 2} + 𝑥 2 + 𝑥 2 (1 − 𝑥 2) ≤ 1 + 𝑥 2 − 𝑥 22 ≤ 5/4.

The point ¯ 𝑥 = (1/2, 1/2) attains this roof bound. Clipping leaves it fixed, with 𝐺 ( ¯𝑥 ) = 1 and 𝐹 ( ¯𝑥 ) = 3/4. Comparing 𝐺 (0, 1/2) = 3/4 and 𝐺 (1, 1/2) = 5/4 chooses 𝑧 = (1, 1/2). Thus 𝐹 (𝑧 ) = 5/4, attaining the certified optimum while retaining a fractional coordinate with negative curvature. Niazadeh et al. [45, Theorem 13] obtain a randomized 1 /2 approximation, up to additive error, for general nonnegative Lipschitz continuous lattice-submodular functions on boxes, using coordinatewise double greedy and zero-sum games. Their class already includes (3.7). For explicit quadratics, the chord reduction and Theorem 3.1 give a shorter construction and proof of the same factor, with an explicit roof certificate. The same reduction can also precede their deterministic DR algorithm [45, Theorem 14]; the simplification here comes from the quadratic representation and the lossless endpoint step. 16 Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du 

3.5 A fixed-terminal cut representation 

For a loopless directed graph 𝐺 = (𝑉, 𝐴 ) with nonnegative arc weights 𝑤 , the directed cut leaving 𝑇 ⊆ 𝑉 has value 

𝛿 + (𝑇 ) :=∑︁  

> (𝑢,𝑣 ) ∈ 𝐴

𝑤 𝑢𝑣 1{𝑢 ∈ 𝑇, 𝑣 ∉ 𝑇 }.

A fixed-terminal directed-cut representation has two terminals 𝑠, 𝑡 , requires 𝑠 ∈ 𝑇 and 𝑡 ∉ 𝑇 , and counts only nonterminal vertices toward the cardinality budget. 

Proposition 3.4 (Fixed-terminal equivalence) . A quadratic submodular pseudo-Boolean function is globally nonnegative if and only if it is the cut-value function of a directed graph with nonnegative arc weights and two fixed terminals. A representation can be constructed in polynomial time, and the cardinality of 𝑆 equals the number of selected nonterminal vertices. Proof. The orientation construction in Section 3.2 gives the nonnegative coefficients in (3.5), with 𝑟 =

min 𝑆 𝑓 (𝑆 ). Introduce arcs 𝑠 → 𝑡 , 𝑖 → 𝑡 , 𝑠 → 𝑖 , and 𝑖 → 𝑗 with weights 𝑟, 𝑝 𝑖 , 𝑞 𝑖 , and 𝜃 𝑖 𝑗 , respectively. The cut leaving {𝑠 } ∪ 𝑆 has value 𝑀 (1𝑆 ) = 𝑓 (𝑆 ). Its number of selected nonterminal vertices is exactly |𝑆 |, so the original cardinality budget is preserved. The orientation program and the graph construction have polynomial encoding length. Conversely, a fixed-terminal directed cut with nonnegative arc weights is a nonnegative sum of 1, 𝑥 𝑖 ,1 − 𝑥 𝑖 , and 𝑥 𝑖 (1 − 𝑥 𝑗 ) after zero-contribution arcs are discarded. Its value function is globally nonnegative, quadratic, and submodular. □

Because the arc weight 𝑟 = min 𝑆 𝑓 (𝑆 ) must be nonnegative, the construction requires nonnegativity on the whole Boolean cube, including infeasible sets. 

Discrete applications. A loopless directed graph with nonnegative arc weights has a multilinear quadratic cut objective of this form. Both an upper cardinality bound and a matroid-independence polytope are down-closed, and Section 10.1 converts the recovered point to a feasible set without further loss. The terminals in Proposition 3.4 enter as constants, so recovery acts only on the nonterminal coordinates. 

3.6 Relation to linear and semidefinite relaxations 

McCormick’s bilinear inequalities provide a basic linearization of quadratic optimization [42]. Goemans and Williamson established semidefinite relaxation and randomized rounding for cut problems [32]; Nesterov studied semidefinite approximation for quadratic models with diagonal quadratic constraints [44]; and Helmberg, Rendl, and Weismantel developed semidefinite bounds and cutting-plane strengthenings for quadratic knapsack [37]. Semidefinite rounding gives stronger guarantees in particular cut models [16, 32]. Our theorem applies to the stated down-closed regions while retaining the continuous diagonal residual. To compare the bounds precisely, we fix the objective and moment constraints of the relaxation below. For the multilinear part, the standard roof has the pairwise form 

𝐿 𝑀 (𝑥 ) = 𝑐 +∑︁

> 𝑖< 𝑗

𝑏 𝑖 𝑗 max {0, 𝑥 𝑖 + 𝑥 𝑗 − 1}.

Here 𝑋 𝑖 𝑗 is a Boolean pair-moment variable, intended to represent Pr (𝑖, 𝑗 ∈ 𝑆 ) for a random set 𝑆 with marginals 𝑥 . At fixed 𝑥 , each pair moment takes its smallest value allowed by the McCormick inequalities. The semidefinite programming (SDP) bound 𝐿 SDP , obtained by adding a positive-semidefinite constraint on the first and second moments while retaining those inequalities, is concave and satisfies 𝑀 ≤ 𝑓 + ≤ 𝐿 SDP ≤ 𝐿 𝑀 .Appendix J gives the formulation and proof. # 4 Cubics: new algebra and two-map recovery 

Quadratics have nonpositive pair coefficients and a separable, concave residual after Boolean multilinearization. Cubics can nevertheless have positive cubic coefficients, and their residual after Boolean multilinearization can fail to be concave. An exact complement identity gives a certificate and concave roof without relying on a concave residual. Two common maps then provide the recovery guarantee. 

4.1 What changes beyond the quadratic decomposition 

Consider the first term of the worked example below: 

𝐹 (𝑥, 𝑦 ) = (1 − 𝑦 )( 2𝑥 − 𝑥 2), 𝑀 (𝑥, 𝑦 ) = 𝑥 (1 − 𝑦 ).

This continuous polynomial is nonnegative and DR-submodular, and 𝑀 extends its Boolean restriction. The residual 𝐹 − 𝑀 = 𝑥 (1 − 𝑥 )( 1 − 𝑦 ) is nonnegative and vanishes at Boolean points, but 

∇2 (𝐹 − 𝑀 ) =

−2(1 − 𝑦 ) 2𝑥 − 12𝑥 − 1 0

The residual is therefore not generally concave. Moreover, the positive coefficient of 𝑥 2 𝑦 is compatible with all the DR inequalities of 𝐹 . A cubic algorithm must address both features.

Proposition 4.3 (Cubic certificate construction) . Every Boolean-nonnegative cubic DR polynomial has a polynomially constructible nonnegative uniform-polarity rooted certificate of literal degree at most three. Its optimally oriented residual constant is its minimum Boolean value. Conversely, every nonnegative sum of constants, literals, and uniform-polarity rooted atoms of literal degree at most three is Boolean-nonnegative and DR-submodular.

Conversely, each uniform-polarity atom has nonpositive diagonal and mixed second partials: its nonlinear part is the negative of a product of literals of one polarity. Nonnegative sums preserve this property and nonnegativity. □

(4.9) Both maps are continuous and downward. The first fixes 1 /2 and maps 1 to zero; the second maps 1 /2 to 1 /3and fixes 1. Downwardness does not require either map to be increasing. 

The two contact configurations. At the midpoint edge, the two branches attain fractions 1 /2 and 4 /9 of the roof value. At the positive cubic atom with root one and two midpoint heads, they attain zero and 8 /9. Weighting the first branch by 𝑝 gives

Balancing these values gives 𝑝 = 8/17 and common value 8 /17. The contact configurations thus determine the weights. To prove the guarantee, however, we must verify the inequality for every occurrence-slot configuration. 

From contact points to the whole domain. For 𝑚 = 1, 2 and 𝜎 ∈ {+ , −} , define the recovery deficit by 

𝐷 𝜎 𝑚 (𝑎, 𝑢 ) = 8ℎ𝜎 𝑚 (𝜙 0 (𝑎 ), 𝜙 0 (𝑢 )) + 9ℎ𝜎 𝑚 (𝜙 1 (𝑎 ), 𝜙 1 (𝑢 )) − 8ℓ𝜎 𝑚 (𝑎, 𝑢 ).

The required scalar inequality is 𝐷 𝜎 𝑚 (𝑎, 𝑢 ) ≥ 0. A knot is a breakpoint between affine pieces; a knot box is a Cartesian product of intervals between consecutive knots. A switching face of an atom roof is the hyperplane where the two affine arguments of its minimum agree. An off-switch region lies on one side of that hyperplane. Within a knot box and a fixed affine branch of the roof, the recovery deficit is affine in each occurrence slot. Moving a minimizing slot to a knot or to the switching face reduces the continuum to knot configurations and switching triangles. Appendix A checks them all. Its triangle certificates use the Bernstein basis, whose nonnegative functions sum to one; nonnegative coefficients therefore certify the inequality. Two triangles need separate arguments because some coefficients are negative. 

Lemma 4.4 (Cubic scalar recovery) . For 𝑚 = 1, 2, 𝜎 ∈ {+ , −} , and all occurrence-slot values, 

8ℎ𝜎 𝑚 (𝜙 0 (𝑎 ), 𝜙 0 (𝑢 )) + 9ℎ𝜎 𝑚 (𝜙 1 (𝑎 ), 𝜙 1 (𝑢 )) ≥ 8ℓ𝜎 𝑚 (𝑎, 𝑢 ). (4.10)

4.9 The same contacts in the sharpness geometry 

The two maps’ midpoint and endpoint values also arise in the cubic sharpness construction. For 0 ≤ 𝑞 ≤ 1and 0 ≤ 𝑡 ≤ 1/2, put

Then 23 − 𝐻 (𝑞, 𝑡 ) = (1 − 𝑞 )( 𝑡 − 1/2)2 + 32 𝑞 (𝑡 − 1/3)2.

Equality holds only at (𝑞, 𝑡 ) = (0, 1/2) and (1, 1/3). Section 8 embeds this expression in a down-closed instance with roof value approaching 17 /12 and lower-box maximum 2 /3. A better feasible point lies outside that box. These equality configurations also constrain higher-degree portfolios in Section 8.4. 

A multilinear application. With three distinct coordinates, 𝑥 𝑖 (1−𝑥 𝑗 𝑥 𝑘 ) is the probability of selecting 𝑖 while not selecting both 𝑗 and 𝑘 . Nonnegative sums of these directed-hyperedge indicators, their complemented counterparts, and unaries form submodular cubic set functions. Apply the cubic recovery theorem to their multilinear extensions and then the rounding guarantee in Section 10. Identifying coordinates produces continuous powers; the scalar proof still applies, but the discrete interpretation changes. 

4.10 Formulation size and bit complexity 

Let 𝑠 be the number of nonzero monomials in a rational polynomial of degree at most three. Each positive cubic monomial in (4.7) generates at most three linear terms and three quadratic terms, in addition to one complemented product and a constant. After collecting coefficients, the negaform therefore has 𝑂 (𝑠 + 𝑛 )

encoded terms. If 𝑟 is its number of nonlinear products and 𝐽 the sum of their distinct support sizes, then 25 Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du 

𝐽 ≤ 3𝑟 . Program (4.4) has 𝐽 + 𝑛 variables, 𝑟 coefficient equalities, and 𝑛 residual inequalities, in addition to nonnegativity. A certificate has at most 𝐽 rooted atoms and 2 𝑛 unaries. Its standard roof uses at most 𝐽 

auxiliary variables and 2 𝐽 affine inequalities; the root-independent form (5.4) can instead use 𝑟 auxiliaries and 2 𝑟 inequalities. At fixed degree, expanding and collecting coefficients preserves polynomial binary encoding length. The determinant bounds for rational linear programs also give an optimal basic solution of the orientation LP with polynomial encoding length [52]. Thus cubic roof optimization is a polynomial-size rational LP over an explicitly encoded polytope. The quadratic hybrid roof adds at most 𝑛 concave diagonal terms; negating it gives a convex quadratic program. Both conclusions also hold under the bounded rational-polytope and strong-separation assumptions in Section 2.5. Feasible optimization to the prescribed additive tolerance, followed by the fixed finite recovery maps, has polynomial bit complexity in the input length and log (1 + 1/𝜀 ). For a general compact convex region, a roof-optimization procedure must be supplied separately. Equation (4.11) bounds the numerical losses. 

# Part II 

# Extensions, recovery boundaries, and discrete consequences 

# 5 Beyond cubics I: larger rooted certificates 

Allowing more literal occurrences in a rooted certificate requires recovery inequalities for products with more heads. We assume that the certificate is supplied, except in the negatively signed subclass, where orientation constructs one. A degree bound alone does not guarantee a certificate. 

5.1 Occurrence-indexed certificates 

To account for repeated variables, we index literal occurrences rather than coordinates. A product has uniform polarity when all its literals are positive or all are complemented; otherwise it has mixed polarity. A literal monomial is a finite product indexed by occurrences

With distinct underlying coordinates, the standard atom roof is the continuous concave envelope; Section 6 gives the coupling argument for uniform polarities. Repeated occurrences can make it nonminimal, as Example 2.2 shows. 

Encoding. A supplied certificate records rational weights, coordinate indices, polarities, and binary multiplicities. An atom roof adds one auxiliary variable and two affine inequalities; multiplicities appear in their coefficients and need not be expanded. Unless a construction is available, the certificate forms part of the input. Objective and map evaluation follow the accuracy assumptions of Section 2.5.

5.3 Mixed heads from uniform-polarity inequalities 

The compatibility rule also permits heads of mixed polarity. Downwardness bounds their recovered product by a complemented-only product, so the complemented slot inequalities suffice. 

Lemma 5.3 (Mixed-polarity domination) . Suppose a downward scalar portfolio pays positive unaries and both slot-atom types through rank 𝑑 − 1 by factor 𝜌 ∈ [ 0, 1]. It then pays every compatible rooted certificate in ℭ≤𝑑 by the same factor. Proof. It remains to treat mixed-polarity atoms. Compatibility forces their root to be 1 − 𝑎 . The recovered head product is therefore no larger than the complemented-only product, and the recovered atom is at least ℎ−

# 6 Beyond cubics II: richer local set functions 

Another extension replaces rooted generators by arbitrary nonnegative submodular functions on small supports. Explicit value tables make their local concave closures tractable, although these functions need not admit the preceding rooted representations. To apply common recovery, we must establish an inequality for this larger local cone. Throughout this section the continuous objective is a multilinear extension.

. (6.5) The root probability and the sum of the head-event probabilities both bound each event probability. To attain the bounds, arrange the head events on a nonatomic probability space so that their union has measure equal to the smaller of one and the sum of their measures. Then arrange the root event to maximize its intersection with this union. Complementing the head events gives the first formula. The standard atom roof therefore equals the exact local closure for distinct variables; Example 2.2 explains why repeated variables require care. 

Proposition 6.1 (Class relations) . For 𝑑 ≥ 2,

R𝑑 ⊆ C 𝑑 ⊆ Q 𝑑 , A𝑑 ⊆ C 𝑑 , R2 = C2 = Q2, R3 = C3 = Q3.

On a four-element ground set, R∞ = R4 ⊊ C4 = Q4. On ground sets of size at least five, C4 and R∞ are incomparable. For 𝑑 < 𝑛 , A∞ ⊈ C𝑑 ; for 𝑛 ≥ 4, A∞ and R∞ are incomparable.

Proof. Every rooted function is nonnegative and submodular, and its degree is at most its arity. Nonnegative sums preserve both properties. The decreasing consecutive differences make concave-cardinality functions submodular. The reverse low-degree inclusions follow from Theorem 4.2 and Proposition 4.3, restricted to multilinear inputs. On four elements, consider the cardinality sequence (0, 2, 3, 3, 3), with polynomial

> 𝑖< 𝑗

𝑥 𝑖 𝑥 𝑗 + 𝑥 1𝑥 2𝑥 3𝑥 4.

Concavity places this function in A4 ⊆ C 4. In a nonnegative uniform-polarity rooted representation, every degree-four monomial has a nonpositive coefficient: a positive rooted product contributes a negative coefficient on its support, and a complemented rooted product contributes a nonpositive coefficient on every four-subset of its support. The positive quartic coefficient therefore excludes R∞, even after embedding the function into a larger ground set. Conversely, 𝑢 𝑇,𝑖 with |𝑇 | = 5 has degree five and cannot belong to C4.The function 𝑆 ↦ → min {1, |𝑆 ∩ 𝐴 |} with | 𝐴 | = 𝑑 + 1 belongs to A𝑑 +1 and has degree 𝑑 + 1, excluding C𝑑 .Finally, 1{1 ∈ 𝑆, 2 ∉ 𝑆 } cannot be a nonnegative sum of concave-cardinality functions. Restrict any proposed sum to {1, 2}. Because the function vanishes at ∅, {2}, {1, 2}, every nonnegative summand must vanish at these sets. A two-coordinate summand has equal values on the two singletons, and a one-coordinate summand takes its selected value also on {1, 2}. Every summand must consequently vanish at {1}, a contradiction. The four-element example proves the reverse noninclusion. □

An extreme ray is a one-dimensional face of a cone. A rational ray has a unique primitive integer generator :the nonzero integer vector on that ray whose coordinates have greatest common divisor one. Relabeling coordinates acts on these generators; a permutation orbit is the set of generators obtained from one another by such relabelings. 

Proposition 6.2 (Recognition at fixed local arity) . For each fixed 𝑑 , membership of a rational degree-at-most-𝑑 

multilinear polynomial in C𝑑 is decidable by a rational linear program of size 𝑛 𝑂 (𝑑 ) . A feasible solution supplies a local conic combination. Proof. For each 𝐴 ⊆ 𝑁 with | 𝐴 | ≤ 𝑑 , enumerate the primitive generators of the cone of nonnegative submodular value tables on 𝐴 . Embed their multilinear coefficient vectors into 𝑁 and match the input coefficients using nonnegative multipliers. For fixed 𝑑 , the number of generators per support is a constant, and there are 𝑂 (𝑛 𝑑 ) supports. The system is feasible precisely when the required decomposition exists. □

For arbitrary encoded arity, a rooted representation can instead be supplied succinctly. Corollary 5.6 gives 8/17 for a supplied representation in R12 , while Theorem 5.4 gives 𝛾 ∗ for R∞ without a degree bound.

Nonnegative aggregation then extends them to local functions with overlapping supports.

The mixed-family corollary therefore uses their shared single map; an 8 /17 guarantee for the union would require a common portfolio. 

# 7 Stronger structure that simplifies recovery 

For globally monotone objectives or concave-cardinality components, the identity map suffices. In the first case, subtracting the modular terms given by each element’s gain when added last (its local terminal marginal) produces monotone factors. In the second, one-dimensional closures and binomial expectations determine the recovery factor. The unary inequality also allows separable concave additions.

7.3 Separable concave additions 

For efficiently represented separable concave terms, subtract each endpoint chord and absorb the chords into the base polynomial. The remaining residual 𝑟 is concave, nonnegative, and satisfies 𝑟 (0) = 𝑟 (1) = 0. If a downward portfolio satisfies Í𝑠 𝑝 𝑠 𝜙 𝑠 (𝑡 ) ≥ 𝜌𝑡 , then, for 𝑡 > 0, ∑︁

𝑡 ∑︁ 𝑠 

𝑝 𝑠 𝜙 𝑠 (𝑡 ) ≥ 𝜌𝑟 (𝑡 ).

Every map vanishes at zero. These concave additions therefore preserve the portfolio guarantees whenever the base polynomial after chord absorption has the required nonnegative certificate and the resulting roof can be optimized efficiently. 

# 8 Sharpness of the specified recovery models 

The sharpness results depend on the roof, benchmark, and permitted recovery rule, as recorded in Table 3. The examples distinguish losses from a fixed roof, representation constraints, incompatible local couplings, and the requirement to use common scalar maps. For termwise portfolios using both polarities, the gap becomes strict at degree thirteen. The first three rows permit arbitrary joint optimization within the lower box. The last requires common scalar maps satisfying a separate inequality for every generator. > 𝐹

≤ 𝐿 R0 for any fixed feasible representation make the roof finite-valued. An infimum of concave functions is concave, so it is a concave majorant. As an infimum of continuous roofs it is upper semicontinuous and thus attains its maximum on every nonempty compact feasible set. The infimum is over the stated distinct-variable uniform-polarity representations. The freedom to change this roof can be expressed as a packing problem. After matching the cubic coefficients, one may add equal positive- and complemented-product mass to a triple only by consuming residual capacity on each of its three pairs. Appendix E characterizes all such changes by a fractional triple-packing system. If the graph of positive residual pair capacities is triangle-free, no triple can receive this extra mass, and every permitted representation induces the same roof. The resulting uniqueness of the roof prevents representation optimization from removing the following obstructions. 

Theorem 8.2 (Representation-optimized boundaries) . The method first maximizes (8.3) over a nonempty compact down-closed set and then may return the best point in [0, ¯𝑥 ], where ¯𝑥 is a roof maximizer. Its largest universal ratio, required to hold for every roof maximizer and measured relative to the true multilinear optimum, is 1/2 for nonnegative submodular polynomials of degree at most two and 8/17 for those of degree at most three.

# 9 Stronger roofs and instance-specific recovery 

We consider two ways to tighten a roof: change the representation, or require local distributions to agree. We develop grouped formulations on supplied tree decompositions, then use finite games and cone duality to choose recovery rules. For a multilinear objective, recovery for a fixed instance reduces to choosing which coordinates to retain. Keeping feasible solutions and valid upper bounds lets us track the strongest guarantee obtained so far. 

9.1 Orientation is not representation choice 

By Proposition 5.2, changing root weights within one exact negaform leaves its standard roof unchanged. Changing the negaform itself can change the roof, even when both representations are exact on the continuous cube. For example, 

𝐹 (𝑥 ) = 2 − 𝑥 1𝑥 2 − 𝑥 1𝑥 3 − 𝑥 2𝑥 3 + 𝑥 1𝑥 2𝑥 3

has the exact identities 

𝐹 (𝑥 ) = 3 − 𝑥 1 − 𝑥 2 − 𝑥 3 − ( 1 − 𝑥 1)( 1 − 𝑥 2)( 1 − 𝑥 3),𝐹 (𝑥 ) = 2 − 𝑥 1𝑥 3 − 𝑥 2𝑥 3 − 𝑥 1𝑥 2 (1 − 𝑥 3).

Compatible nonnegative certificates give the roofs 

𝐿 𝐴 (𝑥 ) = (1 − 𝑥 2) + ( 1 − 𝑥 3) + min {1 − 𝑥 1, 𝑥 2 + 𝑥 3},𝐿 𝐵 (𝑥 ) = (1 − 𝑥 3) + min {𝑥 3, 1 − 𝑥 1} + min {𝑥 3, 1 − 𝑥 2}+ min {1 − 𝑥 3, 2 − 𝑥 1 − 𝑥 2}.

39 Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du 

At 𝑥 = (4/5, 4/5, 1/5), the two roofs equal 6 /5 and 8 /5, respectively. Given a polynomial-size menu of certificates, we can optimize each roof, apply its recovery rule, and keep the best original objective value. The common approximation factor is preserved.

Theorem 9.1 (Nested grouped roofs) . Every 𝐿 Π is concave and Boolean-exact, and 

𝐹 ≤ 𝑓 + ≤ 𝐿 Π′ ≤ 𝐿 Π ≤ 𝐿 whenever Π ⪯ Π′.

The singleton partition gives 𝐿 , and the one-block partition gives 𝑓 +. Any common recovery inequality paying 

𝜌𝐿 also pays 𝜌𝐿 Π and retains its guarantee after optimizing 𝐿 Π .Proof. Mixing feasible fixed-marginal distributions proves concavity, and Boolean marginals force a point mass. Projecting a global distribution onto the block ground sets gives 𝑓 + ≤ 𝐿 Π. When two blocks merge, each expected contribution under a joint distribution is bounded by its separate closure. Maximizing the joint expectation therefore shows that merging cannot increase the roof. The endpoint identities follow from the definitions. Finally, 𝐿 Π ≤ 𝐿 and Theorem 2.3 give the recovery guarantee. □

At fixed marginals, merging preserves the roof value precisely when one joint distribution attains both separate closures. Optimal distributions on disjoint supports can always be coupled. On overlapping supports, they may be incompatible. A direct block formulation uses Í𝐶 2| 𝑁 𝐶 | distribution variables. An alternative exploits the interaction graph, which joins two elements whenever they occur in a common local function. A tree decomposition 

is a tree whose nodes are subsets of graph vertices, called bags. Every vertex belongs to a bag, every edge has both endpoints in one bag, and the bags containing any fixed vertex form a connected subtree. The last condition is the running-intersection property . The width is the maximum bag size minus one. The intersection of adjacent bags is their separator . Separator consistency equates the full joint probability tables on adjacent-bag intersections. 

Proposition 9.2 (Junction-tree formulation) . Given a width-𝜏 𝐶 tree decomposition 𝑇 𝐶 of the interaction graph of block 𝐶 , its exact closure has a linear formulation with 𝑂 (| 𝑇 𝐶 |2𝜏 𝐶 +1) bag-probability variables and separator-consistency constraints. Thus fixed supplied widths yield polynomial-size grouped roof formulations. Proof. Each local ground set is a clique, so it lies in some bag. Assign its objective to one such bag. For each bag, introduce a joint probability table, normalize it, prescribe its singleton marginals, and require adjacent bags to agree on their full intersection tables. The running-intersection property lets us glue these distributions successively into a global law. Conditional laws on zero-probability separator states can be chosen arbitrarily, since they carry no mass. Conversely, every global law induces consistent bag tables. The formulation is therefore exact, and counting table entries gives the size bound [58]. □

40 Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du

A realizable test row consists of a nonnegative component 𝑔 , marginals 𝑥 , a positive roof value 𝑢 , and data proving that value. For a local closure these can be an optimal coupling and a tight affine majorant. Write 𝐺 𝑟 for row

For common-coordinatewise maps, the dual mixture is realizable on disjoint copies of the rows: weighting row 𝑟 by 𝑤 𝑟 /𝑢 𝑟 gives total additive roof one and branch values ( 𝐴 ⊤𝑤 )𝑠 .Proof. The equality is finite linear-programming duality. Independent copies have additive roofs and objectives, and the prescribed weights normalize their roof contributions. □

A finite game certifies only the rows it contains. To obtain a universal guarantee, we need an analytical argument covering the whole domain or exact separation over the generators and marginals. Reweighting fixed maps is a linear program, but choosing their knot values is generally nonconvex. Appendix D describes the certificates used for arity four. Section 9.5 gives a minimax formulation for recovery at fixed marginals.

If one distribution pays every ray, it pays every allowed conic combination: the multilinear extension is linear, while concave closure is subadditive under nonnegative addition. Rows with zero roof value need only a nonnegative contribution, so they can be omitted. Conversely, each positive-roof ray is an allowed local function, making the minimum over rows necessary. Finite linear-programming duality gives the second expression. The stated coefficients make the dual instance’s additive roof equal to one. □

The game may have exponentially many columns. Nevertheless, with rational 𝑥 , fixed arity, explicit local rays, and a supplied bounded-width interaction-tree decomposition, dual separation reduces to maximizing a sum of binary local functions. Tree dynamic programming solves this problem and, under these assumptions, gives polynomial-time optimization of (9.2). Proposition 9.7 (Best-so-far guarantees) . The sequence 𝑈 𝑘 is nonincreasing and 𝑉 𝑘 is nondecreasing. With inexact optimization, the denominator must remain a certified upper bound. A feasible dual value, or a computed roof value plus a valid optimization-error bound, suffices. A primal roof value alone does not bound the optimum from above. Keeping the smallest certified upper bound and the largest feasible objective value preserves monotonicity of the certificate. This retention matters because a tighter roof can have a different optimizer and need not produce a better recovered solution. 

# 10 Discrete optimization and correlation consequences 

For multilinear objectives, rounding converts a recovered point into a feasible set. The same recovery inequalities also bound the ordinary, downward, and max-subset correlation ratios. 

10.1 The rounding interface 

This section assumes that 𝐹 is the multilinear extension of a nonnegative submodular set function 𝑓 , and K

contains every feasible incidence vector for a down-closed family I.

𝜌 (max K 𝐹 − 𝛿 ), and a rounding procedure returns 𝑆 ∈ I with E 𝑓 (𝑆 ) ≥ 𝜂𝐹 (𝑦 ). Then 

E 𝑓 (𝑆 ) ≥ 𝜂𝜌

Proof. Every 1𝑇 , 𝑇 ∈ I , belongs to K, so max K 𝐹 ≥ max 𝑇 ∈ I 𝑓 (𝑇 ). □

A matroid is a nonempty down-closed family satisfying the exchange axiom: if 𝐼, 𝐽 belong to the family and |𝐼 | < |𝐽 |, there is an 𝑒 ∈ 𝐽 \ 𝐼 for which 𝐼 ∪ { 𝑒 } also belongs to the family. Its independence polytope is the convex hull of its incidence vectors. Its maximal independent sets are called bases; they all have the same size, called the rank. Their convex hull is its base polytope. The uniform matroid of rank 𝑘 consists of the sets of size at most 𝑘 .For the cube, successively choosing a maximizing endpoint of each affine coordinate section gives 

𝜂 = 1. For a uniform-matroid independence polytope, or more generally a matroid independence polytope, deterministic pipage rounding also gives 𝜂 = 1 [1, 20]. A zero-value dummy is an added element whose inclusion leaves 𝑓 unchanged. Adjoining enough such elements embeds the fractional point into an enlarged matroid base polytope, by declaring a set feasible when its original elements are independent and its total cardinality, including dummies, is at most the original rank. Along a swap direction 𝑒 𝑖 − 𝑒 𝑗 , the multilinear extension is convex because its second derivative is −2𝜕 𝑖 𝑗 𝐹 ≥ 0. One endpoint therefore does not decrease 43 Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du 

its value; repeated exchanges and deletion of the dummies yield an independent set. Explicit polynomial or local-function representations allow deterministic value comparisons. Swap rounding gives an alternative with no loss in expectation [22]. Independence and at-most-cardinality constraints are down-closed, as recovery requires. Exact-cardinality and base constraints need a separate recovery argument. Rounding also requires additional structure; a separation oracle for an arbitrary down-closed convex set is insufficient. For another independence system, put 𝑃 I = conv {1𝑆 : 𝑆 ∈ I} . A monotone (𝑏, 𝑐 )-balanced contention-resolution scheme , with 0 < 𝑏, 𝑐 ≤ 1, takes 𝑥 ∈ 𝑏𝑃 I and an independent sample 𝑅 𝑥 , and returns a feasible subset. Each sampled element is retained with conditional probability at least 𝑐 , and its retention probability cannot increase as the input sample grows. Given 𝑦 ∈ 𝑃 I , use 𝑥 = 𝑏𝑦 . For a nonmonotone objective, prune the output in a fixed order, retaining an element only if its marginal to the retained set is positive. This gives 

E 𝑓 (𝑆 ) ≥ 𝑐𝐹 (𝑏𝑦 ) ≥ 𝑏𝑐𝐹 (𝑦 ) [23]. The second inequality uses concavity of 𝑡 ↦ → 𝐹 (𝑡𝑦 ) and 𝐹 (0) ≥ 0, so Proposition 10.1 applies with 𝜂 = 𝑏𝑐 .

Corollary 10.2 (Lossless discrete specializations) . for supplied concave-cardinality decompositions. Globally monotone members of C𝑑 have factor 𝛾 𝑑 .Proof. Apply Proposition 10.1 with 𝜂 = 1 to the corresponding roof-recovery results. □

These guarantees allow an arbitrarily small additive loss. Generic unconstrained algorithms attain 1 /2[17]; Corollary 10.2 gives the stated factors under matroid-independence constraints as well.

# Part III 

# Online preservation 

# 11 Online preservation and a fractional prophet interface 

A selectable greedy online contention-resolution scheme can preserve the value of nonnegative sums of directed-hypergraph cuts, unaries, and coverage terms. The proof uses positive association of decreasing events. Combining this preservation result with the benchmark on the full outcome universe in Appendix K gives a prophet guarantee. 

11.1 Greedy online contention resolution 

Let 𝑃 I = conv {1𝑆 : 𝑆 ∈ I} for a nonempty down-closed family I, and fix 𝑏, 𝑐 ∈ [ 0, 1]. An online contention-resolution scheme (OCRS) must accept or reject an active element when it arrives, without changing earlier decisions. For independent activation probabilities 𝑧 , a randomized greedy OCRS first samples a nonempty down-closed family F𝑧 ⊆ I , independently of the active set 𝑅 𝑧 . Thus ∅ ∈ F 𝑧 and the accepted set starts at ∅. It accepts an arriving active element 𝑒 exactly when 𝐽 ∪ { 𝑒 } ∈ F 𝑧 , where 𝐽 is the set accepted so far. Define 

𝑄 𝑒 (𝑅 𝑧 , F𝑧 ) = {𝐽 ∪ { 𝑒 } ∈ F 𝑧 for every 𝐽 ⊆ 𝑅 𝑧 with 𝐽 ∈ F 𝑧 }.

45 Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du 

Adding other active elements can only destroy 𝑄 𝑒 ; toggling the activation of 𝑒 leaves it unchanged. If 𝑒 is active and 𝑄 𝑒 holds, every arrival order leads to its acceptance. The scheme is 𝑐 -selectable if Pr (𝑄 𝑒 ) ≥ 𝑐 for every 𝑒 , and (𝑏, 𝑐 )-selectable if this holds for every 𝑧 ∈ 𝑏𝑃 I , following Feldman et al. [29]. All results below allow an almighty adversary , which observes the active set and the scheme’s random choices before choosing the arrival order.

# Part IV 

# Explicit-input computational hardness 

# 12 Hardness methodology 

Section 8 bounds what specified roofs and recovery rules can achieve. Here we consider computational hardness for explicit quadratic instances. The reductions use terminal-free directed cuts with nonnegative arc weights; Section 3 gives the more general fixed-terminal representation. 47 Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du 

12.1 The explicit cardinality-constrained model 

Let 𝑓 be a globally nonnegative quadratic submodular function as in (3.3), and let 𝐹 denote its multilinear extension. We consider the promised explicit-input problem max { 𝑓 (𝑆 ) : 𝑆 ⊆ [ 𝑛 ], |𝑆 | ≤ 𝑘 }, 𝑓 (𝑆 ) ≥ 0 for every 𝑆 ⊆ [ 𝑛 ]. (12.1) The rational coefficients (𝑐, 𝑎, 𝑏 ) and the integer 𝑘 are encoded in binary. The nonnegativity promise applies to the entire Boolean cube, including sets that exceed the budget. For 0 < 𝛼 ≤ 1, an 𝛼 -approximation returns a feasible 𝑆 with 𝑓 (𝑆 ) ≥ 𝛼 OPT on every promised instance, where OPT denotes the maximum in (12.1). Thus a hardness factor 𝑟 is an upper bound on the attainable approximation ratio: “hard within 𝑟 + 𝜀 ” means that no polynomial-time (𝑟 + 𝜀 )-approximation exists under the stated complexity assumption. All computational-hardness statements here concern deterministic polynomial-time algorithms. The corresponding continuous feasible region is

Incidence vectors give one inequality; rounding any fractional point without decreasing its value gives the other. A continuous approximation on this explicit multilinear subclass therefore yields the same discrete factor. In the normalized reductions below, the additive error can be chosen smaller than the fixed gap between the yes-case and no-case optimum bounds. 

12.2 Test functions and proof mechanism 

A cardinality bound constrains the total selected vertex mass, but different parts of the reduction may have different selection probabilities. A global affine majorant will bound the cut value while allowing this variation. A long-code cloud is a copy of {0, 1}𝑅 , with one coordinate for each label in [𝑅 ] = {1, . . . , 𝑅 }. Its type specifies the product measure, and a Boolean indicator 𝑔 describes the selected part of the cloud. A dictator 

selects a coordinate: 𝑔 (𝑥 ) = 𝑥 𝑖 . A dictatorship test distinguishes these intended indicators from the cloud functions arising in the reduction’s no case. The directed test accepts when its sampled tail is selected and its sampled head is not. Its completeness is the acceptance probability guaranteed by the intended dictators; its 

soundness bounds acceptance in the no case. After replication, we also use fractional cloud indicators in 

[0, 1].

12.3 Retaining directed asymmetry 

If both endpoints have the same distribution and use the same Boolean cloud function, forward and reverse crossings have equal probability. 

Proposition 12.1 (Equal-marginal collapse) . If random variables 𝑋, 𝑌 have identical distributions and 𝑔 is Boolean, then 

P[𝑔 (𝑋 ) = 1, 𝑔 (𝑌 ) = 0] = P[𝑔 (𝑋 ) = 0, 𝑔 (𝑌 ) = 1] = 12 P[𝑔 (𝑋 ) ≠ 𝑔 (𝑌 )] .

48 Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du 

For a one-type test using the same cloud function and bias at both ends, symmetrization multiplies both completeness and soundness by two. The soundness-to-completeness ratio is therefore unchanged whenever completeness is positive. Proof. Since E[𝑔 (𝑋 )] = E[𝑔 (𝑌 )] , subtracting the two crossing probabilities gives zero. Their sum is the disagreement probability. □

With several types, the imbalance between forward and reverse acceptance depends on the cloud means and net flow through each type.

A bipartite Unique-Games instance assigns a permutation of [𝑅 ] to each edge. A labeling assigns one label to each vertex; its value is the fraction of edges whose permutation constraint it satisfies, and val (U) 

is the largest such fraction. We use the regular bipartite form of the Unique Games Conjecture : for every 

𝜁, 𝜅 > 0, some fixed alphabet size 𝑅 makes it NP-hard to distinguish instances of value at least 1 − 𝜁 from those of value at most 𝜅 [5, 39]. Regularity means that degrees are constant on each side, so uniform edge sampling has uniform vertex marginals on each side. The decoding step uses averages over neighboring clouds. Let U be a regular bipartite Unique-Games instance with bipartition (𝑈, 𝑉 ), label set [𝑅 ], and edge permutations 𝜎 𝑢𝑣 . In the reduction, vertices in 𝑈 are the outer vertices , and clouds are attached to vertices in 𝑉 . We use the convention that (𝑢, 𝑣 ) is satisfied when 

ℓ(𝑣 ) = 𝜎 𝑢𝑣 (ℓ(𝑢 )) . For cloud functions 𝑔 𝑣,𝑠 : {0, 1}𝑅 → [ 0, 1], let 𝑔 𝑢 𝑣,𝑠 denote the coordinate permutation of 

𝑔 𝑣,𝑠 induced by 𝜎 𝑢𝑣 , explicitly 𝑔 𝑢 𝑣,𝑠 (𝑥 ) = 𝑔 𝑣,𝑠 (( 𝑥 𝜎 −1    

> 𝑢𝑣 (𝑗 )

) 𝑗 ∈ [ 𝑅 ] ), and define

Lemma 12.5 (Influence decoding) . Fix a finite type system and 0 < 𝜂 < 1. There is a constant 𝜅 > 0,independent of 𝑅 and of the size of U, such that

> E𝑢 ∈𝑈 [Ψ(𝑚 𝑢 )] + 𝜂 =⇒ val (U) ≥ 𝜅. (12.8) 

Proof outline. If acceptance exceeds the Gaussian profile on average, a positive fraction of outer vertices must have a shared influential coordinate. Convexity passes this influence from each neighbor average to a positive fraction of its incident clouds. The total low-degree influence bound limits the number of candidate labels. Choosing labels uniformly from these lists gives a positive expected fraction of satisfied edges. Appendix L.2 gives the constants and permutation convention. □

Theorem 12.6 (Affine finite-type criterion) . Suppose the masses, biases, type weights, and pair-distribution atoms are rational, 𝐶 > 0, and 𝜆 ≥ 0, 𝛽 ∈ R satisfy 

Ψ(𝑧 ) ≤ 𝛽 + 𝜆𝜋 ⊤𝑧 for every 𝑧 ∈ [ 0, 1] T . (12.9) 

Assume also that 𝑆 := 𝛽 + 𝜆𝜇 < 𝐶 . For every 0 < 𝛿 < (𝐶 − 𝑆 )/ 2, the Unique Games Conjecture implies NP-hardness of distinguishing normalized weighted Max-DiCut instances with an at-most-cardinality budget and |𝑘 /𝑛 ′ − 𝜇 | ≤ 𝛿 satisfying 

OPT ≥ 𝐶 − 𝛿 from those satisfying OPT ≤ 𝛽 + 𝜆𝜇 + 𝛿. (12.10) 

Here 𝑛 ′ is the number of vertices and the total arc weight is normalized to one. Consequently, for every 𝜀 > 0

there is no polynomial-time (( 𝛽 + 𝜆𝜇 )/ 𝐶 + 𝜀 )-approximation on this family. Proof outline. The regular long-code construction gives dictator density 𝜇 and completeness approaching 𝐶 .Influence decoding bounds each no-case cut by the average Gaussian profile; the affine inequality bounds that average through the cardinality budget. Rational replication converts vertex masses to ordinary cardinalities. A cut may separate copies of the same vertex, but the resulting fractional cloud indicators obey the same bilinear soundness bound. Appendix L.3 gives the construction and accounts for replication, loop removal, and the error terms. □

12.6 Proof and certificate hierarchy 

The hardness proof needs a valid global affine Gaussian bound, not an optimal one. Proposition 13.2 supplies a computer-assisted bound for the rational two-type test. Theorem 12.6 converts it into a gap, and Theorem 13.3 gives the resulting factor. The constructed nonnegative directed-cut objectives belong to the promised quadratic class by Proposition 3.4. Proposition 12.7 serves the separate task of designing tests: it identifies the exact profile-mixing value obtained by optimizing over affine bounds. 

# 13 Hardness instantiations 

Adding isolated vertices transfers the NP-hardness bound from unconstrained Max-DiCut. The rational two-type test gives a stronger UGC-hardness bound, whose Gaussian certificate we also examine under the losing-half transfer. 52 Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du 

13.1 NP-hardness inherited from Max-DiCut 

Theorem 13.1 (Explicit-input NP-hardness) . For every fixed 𝜀 > 0, it is NP-hard to approximate (12.1) within 

12 /13 + 𝜀 . The statement holds even for weighted Max-DiCut objectives with nonnegative arc weights, no terminals, and budget 𝑘 = 𝑛 ′/2, where 𝑛 ′ is the enlarged ground-set size. Proof. For a weighted directed graph 𝐷 = ([ 𝑛 ], 𝐴 ), its directed-cut objective is 

𝑓 𝐷 (𝑆 ) =∑︁   

> (𝑖, 𝑗 ) ∈ 𝐴

𝑤 𝑖 𝑗 1{𝑖 ∈ 𝑆, 𝑗 ∉ 𝑆 }.

This is an explicit quadratic submodular pseudo-Boolean function that is nonnegative on the whole cube. Trevisan et al. prove that it is NP-hard to approximate unconstrained Max-DiCut within 12 /13 + 𝜀 [56]. Add 𝑛 isolated vertices and set 𝑘 = 𝑛 on the resulting 𝑛 ′ = 2𝑛 vertices. Every original cut now satisfies the budget. Conversely, removing selected isolated vertices preserves its value. The two optima are equal, so an approximation for the constrained instance gives the same factor for the original instance. □

13.3 The losing-half transfer for this test 

For this test, the losing-half composition changes the completeness bound while retaining the certified soundness bound. Define the independent-coordinate value and the certified soundness upper bound by 

𝐼 :=∑︁ 

> 𝑠,𝑡

𝑊 𝑠𝑡 𝑞 𝑠 (1 − 𝑞 𝑡 ), 𝑆 cert := 𝛽 + 𝜆𝜇. 

Exact substitution gives 

𝐶 = 0.143151448765848 , 𝐼 = 0.08648717847 , (13.9) 

𝑆 cert = 0.118590464878885 . (13.10) The strong half-completeness theorem of Bhangale and Khot gives an outer labeling that satisfies every incident constraint at asymptotically half of the relevant vertices [9]. At each such vertex, the sampled endpoint dictators use the same coordinate and contribute 𝐶 . At any other outer vertex, the sampled coordinates can agree or differ. For a type pair, agreement contributes 𝜈 𝑠𝑡 (1, 0) and disagreement contributes 𝑞 𝑠 (1 − 𝑞 𝑡 ).Negative correlation gives 𝜈 𝑠𝑡 (1, 0) ≥ 𝑞 𝑠 (1 − 𝑞 𝑡 ), so the contribution at every such vertex is at least 𝐼 . The completeness lower bound is therefore (𝐶 + 𝐼 )/ 2 − 𝑜 (1). For a left-regular outer instance, assign right-vertex masses according to the distribution induced by a uniformly sampled edge. Rational replication converts 54 Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du 

these masses to ordinary cardinalities as above. For the available soundness bound to certify a gap, one would need 𝑆 cert < (𝐶 + 𝐼 )/ 2. Instead, 

𝑆 cert > 𝐶 + 𝐼 

2 = 0.114819313617924 , 2𝑆 cert 

𝐶 + 𝐼 = 1.032844224 . . . > 1.

The certificate therefore gives no completeness–soundness gap after this conversion. This calculation does not determine the test’s exact soundness or rule out stronger NP-hardness bounds by other means. 

# 14 Further questions 

14.1 Recovery and representation 

We can construct certificates for the NNS subclass, but the other higher-degree guarantees require a supplied compatible certificate. Which further DR-submodular polynomials admit polynomial-size certificates? Corollary G.1 excludes one nonnegative quartic DR polynomial from the compatible rooted cone through literal degree twelve. Representing that polynomial therefore requires a higher literal degree or different generators. What is the exact value of 𝜌 cw 13 , and what do optimal or near-optimal portfolios look like? Theorem 8.4 proves a strict gap below 8 /17 without determining its size. At degree thirteen, the unrestricted joint modulus 

𝜌 joint 13 and the intrinsic recovery ratio relative to the global concave closure also remain open. Which overlap patterns admit stronger common recovery than arbitrary overlap? Can a tractable grouped roof, together with a suitable recovery rule, improve the 8 /17 bound for a class of constrained objectives? 

14.2 Explicit-input hardness 

Improved NP-hardness ratio. Does there exist a constant 𝛿 > 0 for which it is NP-hard to approximate at-most-cardinality Max-DiCut within 12 /13 − 𝛿 ? One possible route is to avoid the certificate failure in (13.9)–(13.10) by a cardinality-aware composition or by optimizing a test directly for the losing-half certificate objective 2 𝑆 cert /( 𝐶 + 𝐼 ).

Optimal finite-type UGC test. For a fixed density 𝜇 , determine inf 





.

Is a two-type construction in which one type mass tends to zero optimal in this class, or do more types and non-extremal pair distributions improve the ratio? 

Bounded-complexity hard instances. Can the ratio in Theorem 13.3 be retained for unweighted bounded-degree directed graphs with a maximum degree bounded independently of the approximation tolerance? 55 Concave roofs and downward recovery Donglei Du

# A Elementary cubic scalar inequalities 

We prove the scalar inequalities used by the cubic algorithm by reducing the slot domain to knot configurations and switching faces. Two cells require separate arguments because their Bernstein coefficients are not all nonnegative. Lemma 4.4 requires the unary inequality together with 8𝜙 0 (𝑎 ) [ 1 − 𝜙 0 (𝑢 )] + 9𝜙 1 (𝑎 ) [ 1 − 𝜙 1 (𝑢 )] ≥ 8 min {𝑎, 1 − 𝑢 }. (A.1) 8𝜙 0 (𝑎 ) [ 1 − 𝜙 0 (𝑢 )𝜙 0 (𝑣 )] + 9𝜙 1 (𝑎 ) [ 1 − 𝜙 1 (𝑢 )𝜙 1 (𝑣 )] ≥ 8 min {𝑎, 2 − 𝑢 − 𝑣 }. (A.2) 8[1 − 𝜙 0 (𝑎 )] [ 1 − ( 1 − 𝜙 0 (𝑢 ))( 1 − 𝜙 0 (𝑣 ))] + 9[1 − 𝜙 1 (𝑎 )] [ 1 − ( 1 − 𝜙 1 (𝑢 ))( 1 − 𝜙 1 (𝑣 ))] ≥ 8 min {1 − 𝑎, 𝑢 + 𝑣 }.

(A.3) The complemented rank-one inequality follows from (A.1) by interchanging the two coordinates. Let 𝐼 𝑗 = [( 𝑗 − 1)/ 4, 𝑗 /4], 𝑗 = 1, 2, 3, 4. Both maps are affine on each interval. Within a product of these intervals and a fixed affine branch of the roof, the recovery deficit defined in Section 4.6 is affine in each independent slot. Move one slot to an endpoint of its feasible interval without increasing the deficit. The endpoint is either a knot or a roof-switching point. Repeating this step shows that any negative deficit must occur with every slot at a knot or on the switching hyperplane. Rational substitution at the five unary knots, 25 pair knots, and 125 triples for each sign gives nonnegative deficits. At knots with zero roof value, atom nonnegativity gives the same conclusion. Each nondegenerate intersection of a knot box with 𝑎 + 𝑢 + 𝑣 = 1 or 𝑎 + 𝑢 + 𝑣 = 2 is a triangle: after translation and scaling by four it is a section of [0, 1]3 with coordinate sum one or two. Write its vertices as 𝑣 0, 𝑣 1, 𝑣 2, in any order, and substitute 𝑣 0 + 𝑟 (𝑣 1 − 𝑣 0) + 𝑡 (𝑣 2 − 𝑣 0), where 𝑟, 𝑡 ≥ 0 and 𝑟 + 𝑡 ≤ 1. > RoofRecovery.theorem_4_10 : Degree12Theorem410Statement

The file RoofRecovery/Atoms.lean defines hPlus , lPlus , hMinus , and lMinus , exactly as in (4.8). Its plusDeficit and minusDeficit are the left side of (5.7) minus its right side. The statement in
